### TC3.3 — The Merger

*A founder's corner office above the boardroom, deal models glowing on a wall screen, a celebration bottle still unopened on the credenza — THE COLLECTOR arrives in a tailored pink suit with a sharp blazer, black pumps, and a gold 222 brooch, her ornate scythe leaned on the boardroom table downstairs showing through the glass floor panel like it owns a seat, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book in her hand, BETTY PLUSH / 222 / PLT running in brass letters along the bookshelf behind his desk.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · KOFI MENSAH (founder, selling his company, calling it a marriage)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
KOFI MENSAH — P 88 · L 41 · T 55 │ PLT 43.6

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
KOFI MENSAH: "If you're from the buyer, the announcement's at four. If you're press, it's still at four."
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm neither, Kofi. I'm the four o'clock nobody scheduled. Open your book with me."
THE COLLECTOR: "Kofi Mensah. Profit eighty-eight. Love forty-one. Tax fifty-five. PLT forty-three point six."
KOFI MENSAH: "Forty-one Love? I built this place from a garage. These people are my family."
THE COLLECTOR: "Were. Family is present tense, darling. Your Love score is written in past. The book don't blink."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 89 (+1) T 22 (—) │ PLT 68.8 ▲
KOFI MENSAH: P 88 (—) L 41 (—) T 57 (+2) │ PLT 42.8 ▼
*Analyst: A founder's favourite tense is the origin story. The Reading drags the numbers into the present, where his Love has been quietly draining while the deal price climbed.*

**Beat 2 — THE MARRIAGE ANNOUNCEMENT**
KOFI MENSAH: "Read the press release. 'A marriage of two great companies.' That's the language we chose. Marriage."
THE COLLECTOR: "A marriage. Lovely. In a marriage, both families get told before the announcement, Kofi."
KOFI MENSAH: "Disclosure rules. We couldn't tell staff before the market."
THE COLLECTOR: "You couldn't tell the market late. There's a difference, and it has forty-one written all over it."
THE COLLECTOR: "Profit eighty-eight says I believe you. Love forty-one asks why the bride's side of the church is empty."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 89 (—) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 69.6 ▲
KOFI MENSAH: P 86 (-2) L 43 (+2) T 57 (—) │ PLT 42.8 ─
*Analyst: He chose the metaphor, so she holds him to it. Calling an acquisition a marriage borrows Love's vocabulary to spend on a Profit event — the book charges the borrowing as Tax.*

**Beat 3 — THE SIDE DOOR**
THE COLLECTOR: "Your head of engineering resigned Tuesday. Your ops lead last week. Did the book imagine that?"
KOFI MENSAH: "People leave during deals. It's normal churn."
THE COLLECTOR: "Love doesn't wait for the announcement — it drains at the speed of rumour."
THE COLLECTOR: "Churn. Another clean word. Your best people aren't churning, Kofi — they're leaving through the side door while you rehearse the front-door speech."
KOFI MENSAH: "I was protecting them from months of anxiety."
THE COLLECTOR: "You were protecting the deal from months of questions. Their anxiety arrived anyway — it just arrived without you in the room to answer for it."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 91 (+2) T 21 (—) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
KOFI MENSAH: P 84 (-2) L 48 (+5) T 53 (-4) │ PLT 45.6 ▲
*Analyst: Silence sold as protection is still silence. His senior staff did the maths before the announcement — Love always leaks ahead of Profit.*

**Beat 4 — PEOPLE UNANNOUNCED**
KOFI MENSAH: "What would you have had me do? Announce the people before the price? The deal could have died."
THE COLLECTOR: "The deal has a data room, a roadshow, and three law firms. The people have a rumour and a bottle you haven't opened."
KOFI MENSAH: "…I wrote the announcement. The price is in paragraph one. The staff are in paragraph nine. 'Valued team members.'"
THE COLLECTOR: "Paragraph nine. In a marriage announcement, the beloved usually makes the headline."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (+1) L 92 (+1) T 20 (-1) │ PLT 71.6 ▲
KOFI MENSAH: P 81 (-3) L 55 (+7) T 45 (-8) │ PLT 50.4 ▲
*Analyst: The order of the paragraphs is the order of the values. He can feel it as soon as she reads his own document back as a ledger: price first, people ninth.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement time. The debt is named plainly: profit announced, people unannounced. You sold the company like a marriage and seated your own family last."
KOFI MENSAH: "The announcement's in two hours. What can still be paid in two hours?"
THE COLLECTOR: "More than you think. Move the people to paragraph one — before the price. Stand in the room yourself, no video message. And before four o'clock, tell your two leads the truth in person, so the side door closes."
KOFI MENSAH: "People first. Price second. …It reads better. God help me, it reads true."
THE COLLECTOR: "There it is. Paid."
THE COLLECTOR: "Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: "Receipt — Nightwing Dawn, tagged and registered. For things that fly at night and still come home. Put it on the credenza, next to the bottle."
THE COLLECTOR: P 75 (+3) L 94 (+2) T 19 (-1) │ PLT 74.0 ▲
KOFI MENSAH: P 78 (-3) L 67 (+12) T 25 (-20) │ PLT 62.0 ▲
*Analyst: The settlement is editorial and physical at once: rewrite the announcement, close the side door in person. The Tax collapses because the secret — the thing charging interest — is spent before the market opens.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.0 · P 75 · L 94 · T 19 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
KOFI MENSAH — PLT 62.0 · P 78 · L 67 · T 25 — ◆◆◆ "PEOPLE IN PARAGRAPH ONE"

**THE MOVE — "THE HEADLINE WEDDING"**
*Kofi Mensah's play: announcing the merger as a marriage and a gift while his staff heard it last, holding the story tight so the people would follow quietly. The maxim: "Announce the price like a vow and the people learn they were only guests at their own wedding." The Collector's answer: PUT THE PEOPLE FIRST — she makes him rewrite the announcement with the people in paragraph one and tell his leads in person before four, so the Settlement hands the story back to the people it was about.*

**Takeaway:** *"Profit announced, people unannounced — a marriage where only the price got an invitation."*

### TC3.4 — The Chair at the Head

*The same boardroom after hours, one lamp lit at the head of the long table, the chair there worn smooth as a throne — THE COLLECTOR arrives in a tailored pink suit with a sharp blazer, black pumps, and a gold 222 brooch, her ornate scythe leaned on the boardroom table like it owns a seat, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book in her hand, BETTY PLUSH / 222 / PLT glowing in the etched glass behind the head chair.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · ALDO REYES (chairman, retired in place for a decade, keeper of the head chair)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
ALDO REYES — P 66 · L 22 · T 58 │ PLT 26.0

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
ALDO REYES: "Young lady, I have sat at the head of this table for thirty years. People make appointments."
THE COLLECTOR: "And for ten of those years, Aldo, the appointment has been the job. Sit — oh. You're already in the chair. Of course you are."
THE COLLECTOR: "Aldo Reyes. Profit sixty-six. Love twenty-two. Tax fifty-eight. PLT twenty-six point zero."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 89 (+1) T 22 (—) │ PLT 68.8 ▲
ALDO REYES: P 66 (—) L 22 (—) T 60 (+2) │ PLT 25.2 ▼
*Analyst: The lowest holding in the chapter belongs to its most senior man. Tenure inflates Profit's story while Love drains — the Reading dates the two separately.*

**Beat 2 — THE EMPTY AGENDA**
ALDO REYES: "My agendas run this company. Every meeting, structured. Every decision, minuted."
THE COLLECTOR: "I've read your agendas. Eleven items, every quarter. Item twelve is the one that never appears."
ALDO REYES: "There is no item twelve."
THE COLLECTOR: "Succession. Ten years of agendas and the word never once made the list. A chair with no item twelve is not a plan, Aldo. It's a lock."
ALDO REYES: "The company is not ready for—"
THE COLLECTOR: "For a name? You don't have to pick the person tonight. You have to admit the chair is a chair, not a limb."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 89 (—) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 69.6 ▲
ALDO REYES: P 64 (-2) L 24 (+2) T 60 (—) │ PLT 25.2 ─
*Analyst: He governs by agenda, so she collects by agenda: the missing item is the debt.*


**Beat 3 — A DECADE OF LATER**
ALDO REYES: "I have said, every year, that I will step back when the time is right."
THE COLLECTOR: "Later. A decade of later. Do you know who pays for later, Aldo?"
ALDO REYES: "Enlighten me."
ALDO REYES: "Talent can wait its turn. I waited mine."
THE COLLECTOR: "You waited for an empty chair. They're waiting under a filled one. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you."
THE COLLECTOR: "The fifty-year-old running your best division, doing the job in everything but title. The forty-year-old who left last spring because the ceiling had your name engraved on it."
ALDO REYES: "…She was good. The one who left in spring."
THE COLLECTOR: "She was succession, walking out with her box, while you were deciding the time wasn't right."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 91 (+2) T 21 (—) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
ALDO REYES: P 62 (-2) L 29 (+5) T 56 (-4) │ PLT 28.0 ▲
*Analyst: 'Later' is the boardroom's most expensive word — its cost lands on people never in the room.*


**Beat 4 — WHO IS WAITING OUTSIDE**
THE COLLECTOR: "There's a woman outside this building who could sit in that chair and raise every score in this book. You interviewed her three years ago."
ALDO REYES: "I remember. Formidable. I said she needed… seasoning."
THE COLLECTOR: "She needed a chair. Seasoning is what we say when the seat is occupied and we'd rather not discuss it."
THE COLLECTOR: "I speak about it like it's the only thing in this room still full of future. Love twenty-two isn't hatred, darling. It's a whole generation's worth of love, unspent, locked in one seat."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (+1) L 92 (+1) T 20 (-1) │ PLT 71.6 ▲
ALDO REYES: P 59 (-3) L 36 (+7) T 48 (-8) │ PLT 32.8 ▲
*Analyst: A kept seat looks like stability from the chair and a locked door from everywhere else. Letting go is the only way his Love gets spent at all.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement time. The debt is named plainly: a seat kept is Love withdrawn from the future. Ten years of holding the head chair so no one younger could sit in it."
ALDO REYES: "And the payment?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Stand up. Not metaphorically — now, in this room, so the book can see it. Then put item twelve on the next agenda, with her name beside it, and your signature under hers."
ALDO REYES: "…Item twelve. Succession. Her name first. Mine underneath, where a signature belongs."
THE COLLECTOR: "There it is. Paid. Look at you — taller standing than sitting. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: "Receipt — Listen, tagged and registered. For the man learning to hear the future knock. Yours, now."
THE COLLECTOR: P 74 (+2) L 95 (+3) T 19 (-1) │ PLT 74.0 ▲
ALDO REYES: P 56 (-3) L 50 (+14) T 26 (-22) │ PLT 46.0 ▲

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.0 · P 74 · L 95 · T 19 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
ALDO REYES — PLT 46.0 · P 56 · L 50 · T 26 — ◆◆ "STOOD UP AT LAST"

**THE MOVE — "THE PERMANENT CHAIR"**
*Aldo Reyes's play: holding the head chair a decade past his season so the future had to ask his permission before it could sit down. The maxim: "Keep tomorrow waiting at the door long enough and tomorrow stops knocking and leaves." The Collector's answer: STAND AND SIGN — she makes him stand from the chair in the room and put succession on the agenda with her name first and his signature underneath, so the Settlement frees the seat instead of just talking about it.*

**Takeaway:** *"A seat kept past its season is Love withdrawn from the future."*

## CHAPTER FOUR — The Bank

### TC4.1 — The Fee Collector

*A marble branch at opening hour, brass rails and a BETTY PLUSH 222 window decal somebody in head office approved and nobody can explain: THE COLLECTOR in a long teal coat, gold 222 chain at her throat, dark glasses pushed up on her head because she is about to read, Drippy the pink drip bunny in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book under one elbow — and her ornate scythe checked at the door like a coat, tag number 222, which the guard handed back to her on sight last time without argument. PLT lettering in brass on the manager's door.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · DEREK VALE (branch manager, a man who has charged a fee for every human event except breathing, and he is working on breathing)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
DEREK VALE — P 62 · L 30 · T 48 │ PLT 31.6

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
DEREK VALE: "We open at nine. Whatever you're selling, the answer is a form."
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm not selling, Derek. I'm reading. Profit sixty-two. Love thirty. Tax forty-eight. PLT thirty-one point six. That is a man who takes money out of every room he walks into and has never once been seen putting any back."
DEREK VALE: "Love thirty? I have a customer satisfaction plaque."
THE COLLECTOR: "You have a plaque. The book is ruder and more accurate. The book don't blink."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 89 (+1) T 22 (—) │ PLT 68.8 ▲
DEREK VALE: P 62 (—) L 30 (—) T 50 (+2) │ PLT 30.8 ▼
*Analyst: THE READING names his Tax before he can invoice it — the moment a hidden cost is read aloud, it starts accruing to the man who hid it.*

**Beat 2 — THE FEE SCHEDULE**
DEREK VALE: "Fees are not theft. Fees are disclosed. Page nine, paragraph four, font size six."
THE COLLECTOR: "A fee you have to hunt for with a magnifying glass isn't a disclosure, Derek. It's a scavenger hunt where you keep the prize."
DEREK VALE: "Every bank does it."
THE COLLECTOR: "Every bank also has a lobby queue for people closing accounts. Yours I counted on the way in — eleven people, not one smiling at the plaque."
DEREK VALE: "The fee schedule is policy."
THE COLLECTOR: "Policy is what a fee calls itself when it doesn't want to give its real name."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (+2) L 89 (—) T 20 (-2) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
DEREK VALE: P 60 (-2) L 32 (+2) T 50 (—) │ PLT 30.8 ─
*Analyst: She doesn't argue the fees are illegal — she argues they buy nothing, and his Profit dips the second the room agrees with her.*

**Beat 3 — PROFIT WITHOUT VALUE**
THE COLLECTOR: "Walk me through one. The paper statement fee. Four dollars."
DEREK VALE: "Printing. Postage. Handling."
THE COLLECTOR: "The statement is generated by a machine, emailed by a machine, and the four dollars is collected by a machine. The only human in that transaction is the pensioner paying it. What did she get, Derek?"
DEREK VALE: "…A statement."
THE COLLECTOR: "She got a bill for the bill. Profit is gain with value under it. Take the value out and what's left is an unnamed Tax wearing a tie. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you. It lands personal, every fee — you just arranged to never be in the room when it lands."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (—) L 91 (+2) T 20 (—) │ PLT 71.2 ▲
DEREK VALE: P 58 (-2) L 36 (+4) T 46 (-4) │ PLT 33.2 ▲
*Analyst: Profit without value is reclassified on the spot — his book loses the Profit and, strangely, gains Love, because being told the truth plainly is the first honest transaction he's had all year.*

**Beat 4 — THE MIRROR**
DEREK VALE: "And what do you do, exactly? You walk in with a — is that a scythe at my door?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Checked. Tag two-two-two. They hand it back without argument. You're the only one who's ever argued, and you argue for a living, so we'll allow it."
DEREK VALE: "You collect souls. I collect fees. Explain the difference to the room."
THE COLLECTOR: "Gladly. When I collect, the debt ends. The person walks out lighter, holding a receipt, owing nothing. When you collect, the debt renews itself monthly and brings friends. I am a settlement. You are a subscription."
DEREK VALE: "…A subscription nobody remembers signing."
THE COLLECTOR: "There it is. Say it again at the settlement and watch your number move."
THE COLLECTOR: P 74 (+2) L 91 (—) T 18 (-2) │ PLT 72.8 ▲
DEREK VALE: P 58 (—) L 40 (+4) T 40 (-6) │ PLT 37.2 ▲
*Analyst: THE MIRROR is the whole conversation — two collectors, one difference: hers terminates the debt, his compounds it. He sees it. Seeing it is worth six points of Tax.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "The debt, plainly. Profit taken without value, itemised at last: eleven years of fees for nothing, charged to people who trusted the brass on your door. The Tax was never theirs, Derek. It was yours, billed out by the month. I collect it now — all of it, once, and then it stops."
DEREK VALE: "And what do I do on Monday?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Print the fee schedule in font size twelve and tape it to the window next to my decal. Charge for real things. Watch the queue."
DEREK VALE: "It'll be shorter."
THE COLLECTOR: "It'll be smiling. Settlement receipt — Pixel Heart, tagged and exact, Betty Plush issue: small pieces, honestly assembled, still make a whole heart. Carry it where the plaque can see it. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 74 (—) L 93 (+2) T 16 (-2) │ PLT 74.4 ▲
DEREK VALE: P 56 (-2) L 48 (+8) T 24 (-16) │ PLT 46.0 ▲
*Analyst: THE SETTLEMENT takes sixteen points of Tax off him at once — a debt billed out monthly for eleven years, collected plainly, turns out to be payable after all.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.4 · P 74 · L 93 · T 16 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
DEREK VALE — PLT 46.0 · P 56 · L 48 · T 24 — ◆◆ "THE SUBSCRIPTION, CANCELLED"

**THE MOVE — "THE SMALL PRINT HARVEST"**
*Derek Vale's play: He buried fees in font size six, small enough that no single charge was worth a fight and steady enough, across thousands of accounts, to run the branch on profit taken without value. The maxim: "Take a sum too small to argue with from enough people who are too tired to argue, and a fortune can leave the room without ever raising its voice." The Collector's answer: THE FONT-SIZE-TWELVE READING — She reads the hidden schedule aloud, names each valueless fee as Tax, and collects eleven years of it in one plain settlement instead of a monthly skim.*

**Takeaway:** *"A fee with no value under it is just a Tax that learned to wear a tie."*

### TC4.2 — The Locked Account

*Counter three, behind the glass, a BETTY PLUSH standee by the deposit slips and 222 stencilled on the queue rope: THE COLLECTOR in a long teal coat, gold 222 chain catching the fluorescent light, dark glasses pushed up on her head because she is about to read, Drippy the pink drip bunny in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book open on the counter ledge — her ornate scythe checked at the door like a coat, tag 222, and the guard already reaching to hand it back on her way out. PLT etched small on the teller's nameplate, under the name.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · JUNE OKAFOR (teller, nine years at counter three, who has never once broken a rule out loud)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
JUNE OKAFOR — P 45 · L 62 · T 55 │ PLT 34.8

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
JUNE OKAFOR: "I can help the next person in line. That's you, apparently. Do you have an account with us?"
THE COLLECTOR: "I have an account of you, June. Listen to it. Profit forty-five. Love sixty-two. Tax fifty-five. PLT thirty-four point eight. That is a kind woman — Love sixty-two is real, the book doesn't flatter — paying a Tax that eats nearly everything her kindness earns."
JUNE OKAFOR: "Fifty-five? From what? I don't owe anybody anything. I checked. I check everything. It's my job to check."
THE COLLECTOR: "You owe one person, and you see her every Sunday, and every Sunday the interest comes due at the dinner table."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 89 (+1) T 22 (—) │ PLT 68.8 ▲
JUNE OKAFOR: P 45 (—) L 62 (—) T 58 (+3) │ PLT 33.6 ▼
*Analyst: THE READING finds the debt in the one ledger June never audits — her own family — and her Tax rises three points just hearing where to look.*

**Beat 2 — POLICY SAYS**
JUNE OKAFOR: "If this is about my mother's account — that was policy. The signature didn't match the card on file. Policy says freeze. I froze it. Any teller would have."
THE COLLECTOR: "Any teller would have frozen it on the Tuesday. Name me the teller who would still have it frozen three years later."
JUNE OKAFOR: "She never filed the reinstatement form."
THE COLLECTOR: "She asked her daughter to help her file it. Her daughter works at counter three, June, and said — quoting your own book — 'Mum, you know I can't touch family accounts, it's policy.'"
JUNE OKAFOR: "It IS policy."
THE COLLECTOR: "The freeze was policy. The three years were you. Policy was the door. You were the one who kept standing in it. The book don't blink, June."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 89 (—) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 69.6 ▲
JUNE OKAFOR: P 44 (-1) L 64 (+2) T 58 (—) │ PLT 34.0 ▲
*Analyst: She separates the rule from the hiding — the freeze was one day of policy, but the hiding has been a thousand days of June, and separating them is the first crack in the Tax.*

**Beat 3 — EVERY FAMILY DINNER**
THE COLLECTOR: "Tell me about Sunday dinner."
JUNE OKAFOR: "…She cooks. I bring plantain. She says, 'My money is still sleeping in your bank, June.' She says it like a joke."
THE COLLECTOR: "And you laugh like it's a joke, then drive home doing arithmetic nobody assigned you. Three years of Sundays, June — you've paid this Tax a hundred and fifty-six times and the account is still frozen. A debt you pay forever and never reduce isn't a payment plan. It's a hiding place with a schedule."
JUNE OKAFOR: "If I unfreeze it now, she'll know it could have been unfrozen all along."
THE COLLECTOR: "She knows. Mothers always know. She's not waiting for the form, love. She's waiting to find out whether her daughter is braver than her rulebook."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 91 (+2) T 21 (—) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
JUNE OKAFOR: P 44 (—) L 68 (+4) T 52 (-6) │ PLT 38.0 ▲
*Analyst: Naming the hiding place drops her Tax six points — the dinner-table dread was never about the money, and the book proves it by moving before a single form is filed.*

**Beat 4 — THE UNFREEZE**
JUNE OKAFOR: "I can't process family accounts. That part really is in the manual. Page —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Page nineteen, I know. You cannot process it. Can you walk it to Derek's office, put it on his desk, and say 'this one is my mother, and it has waited long enough'?"
JUNE OKAFOR: "…That's not processing. That's escorting."
THE COLLECTOR: "Escorting is allowed. Escorting is just love with its shoes on. And a manager's signature outranks a freeze older than some of the staff."
JUNE OKAFOR: "He'll ask why it sat for three years."
THE COLLECTOR: "Tell him a rule hid a daughter, and the daughter has come out. Derek is having his own morning, June. Today of all days, that man will sign."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (+2) L 91 (—) T 19 (-2) │ PLT 72.0 ▲
JUNE OKAFOR: P 46 (+2) L 72 (+4) T 44 (-8) │ PLT 43.6 ▲
*Analyst: THE UNFREEZE is a route, not a violation — she finds the lawful path June's fear never looked for, and eight points of Tax fall off before the form even moves.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "The debt, plainly. A rule you hid behind for three years while your mother's money slept and your Sundays bled. The rule was real. The hiding was the Tax, and the Tax is what's due. I collect the hiding now — not the teller, not the daughter, just the hiding."
JUNE OKAFOR: "And the account?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Walk it down the hall before lunch. Then call your mother from Derek's office phone, on speaker, and let a branch manager tell her the money is awake. She'll cry. Let her. Crying is a deposit."
JUNE OKAFOR: "And this is for — is that for me?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement receipt — Bell Paws, tagged and exact, Betty Plush issue. Soft paws, June — for hands about to pick up a form they've feared for three years. If they shake anyway, it won't tell. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (—) L 94 (+3) T 17 (-2) │ PLT 74.0 ▲
JUNE OKAFOR: P 46 (—) L 80 (+8) T 28 (-16) │ PLT 53.2 ▲
*Analyst: THE SETTLEMENT collects the hiding and leaves the woman — Tax down sixteen, Love up eight. The account is almost an afterthought. Almost. She'll still escort it before lunch.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.0 · P 73 · L 94 · T 17 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
JUNE OKAFOR — PLT 53.2 · P 46 · L 80 · T 28 — ◆◆ "OUT FROM BEHIND THE RULE"

**THE MOVE — "THE RULEBOOK UMBRELLA"**
*June Okafor's play: She froze her mother's account under a real policy on one Tuesday, then kept standing under that rule for three years so the rule, not her hand, would seem to be the thing keeping the money asleep. The maxim: "A true rule lifted over a frightened heart can keep off every hard question, even the ones asked by your own mother." The Collector's answer: THE LAWFUL ESCORT — She separates the one day of policy from the thousand days of hiding, then walks the form lawfully to the manager's desk so love moves without breaking a single rule.*

**Takeaway:** *"A rule can freeze an account in a day. Only hiding keeps it frozen for three years."*

### TC4.3 — The Loan That Ate the House

*A glass-walled lending office above the lobby, BETTY PLUSH 222 in frosted film across the glass and PLT in small brass under the lending licence: THE COLLECTOR in a long teal coat, gold 222 chain over a dark rollneck, dark glasses pushed up on her head because she is about to read, Drippy the pink drip bunny in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book laid flat on the loan officer's desk like a file he forgot he signed — her ornate scythe checked at the door like a coat, tag 222, handed back on sight, no argument, which is more than his borrowers ever got.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · MARCUS REED (loan officer, fourteen years of approvals, a wall of framed volume awards and no photographs of houses)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
MARCUS REED — P 78 · L 28 · T 60 │ PLT 32.4

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
MARCUS REED: "If you're here about a loan, I should tell you, our rates are very competitive."
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm here about several hundred, Marcus, but let's start with yours. Profit seventy-eight. Love twenty-eight. Tax sixty. PLT thirty-two point four. Seventy-eight is the highest Profit in this building — and sixty is the highest Tax, and the book insists both of those are the same fact."
MARCUS REED: "Tax sixty? My taxes are handled. I have a guy."
THE COLLECTOR: "Not the revenue kind, love. The kind you booked to other people's addresses. Yours has been compounding at their kitchen tables for fourteen years."
MARCUS REED: "I don't follow."
THE COLLECTOR: "You will. The book is patient and I am extremely followable."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 88 (—) T 22 (—) │ PLT 68.8 ▲
MARCUS REED: P 78 (—) L 28 (—) T 63 (+3) │ PLT 31.2 ▼
*Analyst: THE READING puts his Profit and his Tax in the same sentence for the first time — his Tax rises three points the moment he hears they're one number wearing two suits.*

**Beat 2 — THE MARKET SAYS**
MARCUS REED: "Loans perform or they don't. That's the market. I don't set rates, I don't set prices, I don't set the economy. I process applications."
THE COLLECTOR: "The market. Tell me, Marcus — when the market walks into this office, does it have a face? Does it sit in that chair and cry at month nineteen?"
MARCUS REED: "Applicants sign disclosures."
THE COLLECTOR: "Applicants sign what you slide across this desk at the speed of a man who knows the variable rate better than they do. 'The market' is a beautiful phrase. The market has no desk, no nameplate, and — this is my favourite part — it can never be collected. That's why you rent it your signature."
MARCUS REED: "You think I invented variable rates?"
THE COLLECTOR: "No. I think you knew which ones would eat the houses, and you said 'the market' the way other men say 'the dog ate it.' Except the dog, in this case, has your initials in the approval box."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (+2) L 88 (—) T 20 (-2) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
MARCUS REED: P 75 (-3) L 30 (+2) T 63 (—) │ PLT 30.8 ▼
*Analyst: "The market" is his unnamed Tax's alibi — an author who cannot be served papers. Stripping the alibi costs him Profit and buys him the first two points of Love he's earned in the book: somebody finally talked to him like an author.*

**Beat 3 — OTHER PEOPLE'S TAX**
THE COLLECTOR: "Page: the third approval of a wet November. You knew the payment would double. You knew the overtime that covered it had already ended. What did you write in the notes?"
MARCUS REED: "…'Borrower optimistic.'"
THE COLLECTOR: "Borrower optimistic. Two words. You took a family's whole Tax — the dread, the arrears letters, month nineteen in that chair — and booked it as your Profit, because your Profit only goes up when somebody else's Tax does. That's not lending. That's moving weight from your page to theirs and charging them for the courier. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you."
MARCUS REED: "The volume awards —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Are on the wall behind you. Fourteen frames. I notice there are no photographs of houses, Marcus. In fourteen years of lending, shouldn't there be houses?"
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (—) L 90 (+2) T 20 (—) │ PLT 71.2 ▲
MARCUS REED: P 72 (-3) L 34 (+4) T 57 (-6) │ PLT 33.6 ▲
*Analyst: The transfer is named — other people's Tax, booked as his Profit — and naming a transfer reverses a little of it on the spot: Tax down six, Love up four, because a ledger with an author can finally be settled.*

**Beat 4 — THE HOUSE REMEMBERS**
MARCUS REED: "What do you want me to do? Un-approve fourteen years? Those houses are gone. Some of those families — I don't even know where they went."
THE COLLECTOR: "The house remembers, even if the officer forgets. And you're not asked for fourteen years today. You're asked for the next file. It lands tomorrow, carried by a man whose overtime has already ended, and your pen will hover — and in the hover you'll hear a voice suspiciously like mine: write what you know."
MARCUS REED: "'Borrower optimistic.'"
THE COLLECTOR: "Write 'officer declines.' Two words. Same length. Opposite direction. One pair fed a wall of frames. The other feeds a family you've never met, in a house that gets to stay a house."
MARCUS REED: "My volume —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Will dip. Your Tax will fall off a cliff. Choose which graph you'd rather be buried under."
THE COLLECTOR: P 75 (+2) L 90 (—) T 18 (-2) │ PLT 72.8 ▲
MARCUS REED: P 70 (-2) L 40 (+6) T 48 (-9) │ PLT 38.8 ▲
*Analyst: She doesn't demand restitution he can't make — she prices the next decision, and his Love jumps six points because "officer declines" is the first sentence he's ever imagined writing for somebody else's benefit.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "The debt, plainly. Other people's Tax, booked as your Profit, for fourteen years, under an alibi with no desk. The houses are gone and the book doesn't pretend otherwise — it never sells a resurrection. What's collectable is the alibi, and the Tax it hid. I take both now. What remains is a loan officer — a respectable thing to be, once it's the officer lending and not the weather."
MARCUS REED: "And tomorrow's file?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Arrives at nine. Your pen hovers. You know the rest — you wrote it in Beat Four, out loud, where the book could hear it. That's a signature too, Marcus. The first honest one in the frame collection."
MARCUS REED: "…Officer declines."
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement receipt — Ember Stripe, tagged and exact, Betty Plush issue. A stripe of fire, carried warm. Put it where the applications land, so every borrower sees one thing in this office that was given, not sold. Then decline beautifully. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 76 (+1) L 92 (+2) T 16 (-2) │ PLT 74.8 ▲
MARCUS REED: P 68 (-2) L 50 (+10) T 30 (-18) │ PLT 49.2 ▲
*Analyst: THE SETTLEMENT collects the alibi, not the man — eighteen points of Tax gone, the chapter's largest drop: a Tax farmed out to hundreds of addresses was always addressed to one desk.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.8 · P 76 · L 92 · T 16 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
MARCUS REED — PLT 49.2 · P 68 · L 50 · T 30 — ◆◆ "THE OFFICER, DECLINING BEAUTIFULLY"

**THE MOVE — "THE WEATHERMAN'S ALIBI"**
*Marcus Reed's play: He approved loans he knew would double and eat the houses, then called every consequence "the market" so no person — least of all the officer whose initials sat in the approval box — ever had to be the one who did it. The maxim: "Call a man's decision weather, and nobody looks for the hand that made it rain." The Collector's answer: THE SIGNATURE RESTORED — She strips the weather-words away, puts his authorship back on the page, and prices the next file in advance with two honest words: "officer declines."*

**Takeaway:** *" 'The market' is the name a man gives his Tax when he wants somebody else to pay it."*

### TC4.4 — The Vault

*Down the brass stairs to the vault floor, quiet as a chapel, a small BETTY PLUSH 222 plaque by the time-lock that the night staff polish without being asked: THE COLLECTOR in a long teal coat, gold 222 chain soft against the collar, dark glasses pushed up on her head because she is about to read — gently, this time — Drippy the pink drip bunny in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book held a little lower than usual, like something you carry into a sickroom. Her ornate scythe is checked at the door upstairs like a coat, tag 222. Down here she would not have brought it anyway. PLT in faint brass on the vault door, worn by forty years of one man's hands.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · OLD MR. ABERNATHY (retired vault guard, forty years on this floor, keeper of one box that does not exist)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
MR. ABERNATHY — P 38 · L 82 · T 68 │ PLT 34.8

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
MR. ABERNATHY: "Vault closes at four. Has done since before a lot of things."
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm not here for the vault, Mr. Abernathy. I'm here for a page in my book with your name on it, and I'll read it the way it deserves. Profit thirty-eight. Love eighty-two. Tax sixty-eight. PLT thirty-four point eight."
MR. ABERNATHY: "Love eighty-two?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Eighty-two. I can count the Love scores above eighty I've ever read on one hand, and one of the fingers is yours. The Tax is the problem — sixty-eight is a heavy coat to carry down these stairs every day."
MR. ABERNATHY: "I don't come down every day. I'm retired."
THE COLLECTOR: "You come down every day, Mr. Abernathy. They keep your chair by the vault door oiled. Retired is a word. The book has the stairs."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 90 (+2) T 22 (—) │ PLT 69.2 ▲
MR. ABERNATHY: P 38 (—) L 82 (—) T 70 (+2) │ PLT 34.0 ▼
*Analyst: THE READING is done softly here — the book leads with his Love, because with a man like this you open on what he carried well before you name what he couldn't carry at all.*

**Beat 2 — FORTY YEARS OFF THE BOOKS**
THE COLLECTOR: "Box seven-one-nine doesn't exist. It's not in the register, it never was, and for forty years it has been the most faithfully guarded box in this building. Guards rotate, managers rotate. The box stayed. You stayed."
MR. ABERNATHY: "A guard doesn't open boxes. That's the first thing they teach you. A guard doesn't open boxes."
THE COLLECTOR: "You never opened it. I know. That's not discipline, Mr. Abernathy — that's a man standing watch over a door he's also locked from the inside."
MR. ABERNATHY: "It's hers. Letters. She wrote like other people breathe, that woman. Every trip, every anniversary, every nothing-Tuesday. After — the solicitor wanted an inventory. Everything listed. Everything priced. I couldn't—"
THE COLLECTOR: "You couldn't say the number of her out loud to a man with a clipboard. So you gave her a box with no number at all. Seven-one-nine. The register skips it like a held breath."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 92 (+2) T 22 (—) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
MR. ABERNATHY: P 38 (—) L 84 (+2) T 70 (—) │ PLT 34.8 ▲
*Analyst: Forty years off the books was not a crime in this ledger — it was custody. The box was hidden not from the bank but from the pricing of a life, and the book scores the guarding as Love.*

**Beat 3 — THE LETTERS**
MR. ABERNATHY: "I read the top one. Once. The night of the funeral. From the seaside. She says the hotel towels were stolen-worthy and my snoring kept the gulls honest. I laughed. At the graveside I'd forgotten I knew how, and there it was in an envelope."
THE COLLECTOR: "And the rest?"
MR. ABERNATHY: "Unopened. Forty years of unopened. If I read them, they run out. A letter you've read is finished. A letter you haven't — she's still writing it. Somewhere in that box she's still mid-sentence."
THE COLLECTOR: "Oh, Mr. Abernathy. Listen carefully, because this is the truest thing in my book and I checked it twice. She is not mid-sentence. She finished every letter, sealed them, and sent them to the only address she trusted. A letter isn't finished when it's read, sir. It's finished when it's delivered. They were all delivered. You've stood guard over a completed delivery for forty years, afraid opening it would end it — and the ending was the one part she'd already handled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 93 (+1) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 71.2 ▲
MR. ABERNATHY: P 39 (+1) L 88 (+4) T 64 (-6) │ PLT 39.2 ▲
*Analyst: THE LETTERS reframes the vault — he isn't hoarding her words, he's postmarking them forever. Telling him the delivery is complete takes six points of Tax off him.*

**Beat 4 — GRIEF, NAMED**
THE COLLECTOR: "Shall I tell you what the sixty-eight actually is? It isn't the box. It isn't even missing her — missing her is in the Love column, where it belongs, at eighty-two and rising. The Tax is the grief you never named. You called it duty. You called it 'keeping busy,' which is what grief calls itself when it clocks in wearing a uniform."
MR. ABERNATHY: "A man my age — we weren't given the word for it. We were given the stairs, and the chair, and the register."
THE COLLECTOR: "Then take the word now, with the Agency's compliments. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you — even this one, sir, especially this one. It's grief, Mr. Abernathy. It was always grief. The oldest unnamed Tax in my entire book — and the only one that shrinks simply by being called by its right name in a quiet room."
MR. ABERNATHY: "Grief."
THE COLLECTOR: "Once more. The vault has wonderful acoustics; it's heard everything else."
MR. ABERNATHY: "…Grief. There. It's out. It sounds smaller outside me than it did inside."
THE COLLECTOR: "It always does, sir. That's the whole of my profession in one sentence — and you just said it better than the book."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (+1) L 95 (+2) T 20 (-1) │ PLT 72.8 ▲
MR. ABERNATHY: P 40 (+1) L 92 (+4) T 56 (-8) │ PLT 44.4 ▲
*Analyst: GRIEF, NAMED is the chapter's heart — no trick, no mirror, no fee schedule. Just the oldest Tax in the book spoken aloud twice in a vault, and eight points of it leave with the echo.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "The debt, plainly — and it is the gentlest one I have ever collected. Grief, unnamed for forty years, paid in daily instalments on a flight of brass stairs by a man who thought he was just doing a job. I collect the unnamed part now. The grief itself stays yours — I don't take love's evidence, Mr. Abernathy, not ever. What I take is the hiding it had to wear to get into this building."
MR. ABERNATHY: "And the box?"
THE COLLECTOR: "The box goes home. Tonight. It sits by your chair, and you read one letter with your tea — one, mind, there's no schedule; the schedule was the Tax — and when you laugh at the towels, and you will, that's not the end of her. That's the delivery, arriving. Forty years late is still arriving. She addressed them to you, sir. Not to a vault."
MR. ABERNATHY: "And what's this? I can't take a — I'm a grown man, I have a — oh. Oh, it's soft."
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement receipt — Mendie, tagged and exact, Betty Plush issue. Mendie mends. She sits on the box lid at home, where a guard used to stand, so the letters have company that doesn't need a register. Forty years you kept watch, Mr. Abernathy. The watch is over. The letters are yours, the word is yours, and the stairs, from tomorrow, are just stairs. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (—) L 97 (+2) T 18 (-2) │ PLT 74.4 ▲
MR. ABERNATHY: P 40 (—) L 98 (+6) T 40 (-16) │ PLT 53.2 ▲
*Analyst: THE SETTLEMENT collects only the hiding — Tax down sixteen, his Love finishing at ninety-eight, the book's highest. Some numbers you don't beat. You witness them.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 74.4 · P 72 · L 97 · T 18 — ◆◆◆◆ "ALWAYS RIGHT ABOUT THE NUMBER"
MR. ABERNATHY — PLT 53.2 · P 40 · L 98 · T 40 — ◆◆ "THE WATCH IS OVER"

**THE MOVE — "THE SILENT CUSTODY"**
*Mr. Abernathy's play: He kept one box — box 719, his late wife's letters — off the register for forty years, hiding it from the bank's inventory and from his own grief, because an unopened letter could still feel like she was mid-sentence. The maxim: "What you keep off every ledger to spare it a price will still be priced, quietly, in the years you spend standing guard." The Collector's answer: THE GENTLE DELIVERY — She collects only the hiding, names the grief aloud in the quiet vault, and sends the box home to be read, because a letter is finished when it is delivered, not when it is locked away.*

**Takeaway:** *"A letter isn't finished when it's read. It's finished when it's delivered — and grief, named, is a delivery too."*

CARLA MENDEZ: "That is not a hospital number."
THE COLLECTOR: "No. It is the one underneath all of them."

**Beat 2 — THE INVOICE FOR THE INVOICE**

THE COLLECTOR: "You itemised a charge for sending the charge."
CARLA MENDEZ: "That is a processing fee."
THE COLLECTOR: "That is an invoice for the invoice, Carla. Even the paper is embarrassed."
CARLA MENDEZ: "I do not invent the codes."
THE COLLECTOR: "No. You just never read them in a human voice. Try this one. Out loud. To me."

**Beat 3 — READ IT ALOUD**

THE COLLECTOR: "Again. Like the woman in bed four is your sister."
CARLA MENDEZ: "'We charged you for organising your care... while you were asleep.'"
THE COLLECTOR: "There she is."
CARLA MENDEZ: "I have sent that line four thousand times. I have never once heard it."
THE COLLECTOR: "A tax nobody will say out loud does not disappear. It just collects interest in the dark. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you."

THE COLLECTOR: "Tell them the Collector did. We have excellent letterhead."
CARLA MENDEZ: "And the statements already in beds upstairs? People reading numbers bigger than the treatment, while they are still in the bed."
THE COLLECTOR: "Then we write a new first page. Plain words. What was done, what it cost, who to call when it frightens you."
CARLA MENDEZ: "That page would be longer."
THE COLLECTOR: "Fear is longer, Carla. I have measured both."

CARLA MENDEZ: P 65 (+3) L 63 (+5) T 24 (-5) │ PLT 55.6 ▲
*Analyst: This is policy as a love move — a plain-language first page redeposits care into a system that withdrew it, and Carla starts spending her authority on people instead of protection.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**

THE COLLECTOR: "The Tax you itemised for strangers and never once read aloud. That is the whole debt. Say the surcharge is gone, and it is collected."
CARLA MENDEZ: "The surcharge is gone. As of tonight, no one in this building pays to be organised in their sleep."
THE COLLECTOR: "Collected. And here — your receipt. Pixel Heart, tagged and registered. A small Betty Plush soul for a clean page."
CARLA MENDEZ: "She has a stitched heart."

**THE MOVE — "FLUENT IN EVERY STRANGER'S BILL"**
*Carla Mendez's play: For twelve years Carla itemised strangers' pain to the cent while never once reading her own page aloud, fluent in every account in the building except hers. The maxim: "A truth you can price for others but cannot say to yourself will keep charging you for the silence." The Collector's answer: READ YOUR OWN PAGE ALOUD — the Reading puts Carla's holding in her own flat billing voice, and the Settlement has her speak and strike the surcharge, so her page is heard at last.*

**Takeaway:** *"Name the charge out loud, and it stops charging you in secret."*

**THE MOVE — "ALWAYS THE PAIR OF HANDS"**

### TC5.4 — The Discharge Papers

*The discharge lounge at midday, a printer running warm, a stack of folders moving at impressive speed across the desk: THE COLLECTOR in a soft lavender-and-white fit, quiet colours, white sneakers, her scythe carried soft with its blade veiled in pink cloth and propped where no one will trip on it, Drippy the pink drip bunny held closer than usual in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book placed calmly on top of the fastest folder in the pile, BETTY PLUSH 222 on the lounge's small pink sign and PLT printed neatly on the top sheet.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · DR. PRIYA NAIR (young doctor, brilliant diagnostician, signer of discharge papers at speed)

**OPENING HOLDING**

THE COLLECTOR: "Neither, Doctor. Priya Nair — Profit eighty-four, Love fifty-five, Tax thirty-nine. PLT fifty-four point zero."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Profit eighty-four? I am the fastest discharge on this floor."
THE COLLECTOR: "That is exactly what the eighty-four says. It is the fifty-five I have come about."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Love is not on the discharge form."
THE COLLECTOR: "No. That is rather the entry I am auditing."

DR. PRIYA NAIR: P 83 (-1) L 58 (+3) T 37 (-2) │ PLT 55.6 ▲
*Analyst: The Reading honours her brilliance first — Profit 84 is real — then isolates the gap: a Love score thirty points behind her competence is the whole case.*

**Beat 2 — ANY QUESTIONS**
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "I say it every time. 'Any questions?' It is on my checklist."
THE COLLECTOR: "Play me the last one. Exactly as it sounded."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "'Meds twice daily, no driving for a week, anyquestions?' ...That is not a question. That is a door closing with words on it."
THE COLLECTOR: "When did you last hear yourself say it slowly?"
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Residency. Maybe. Before the speed became the thing I was praised for."
THE COLLECTOR: "Speed is a fine servant, Doctor. It makes a terrible signature."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 92 (+3) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 70.8 ▲
DR. PRIYA NAIR: P 83 (—) L 63 (+5) T 34 (-3) │ PLT 58.8 ▲
*Analyst: Hearing her own hurried 'anyquestions?' played back is the diagnosis — love withdrawn not by coldness but by hurry, one compressed word at a time.*

**Beat 3 — THE SPEED OF SORRY**
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "You think I do not care. I stay late. I check results from home."
THE COLLECTOR: "I think you care at a speed no patient can receive. Care that arrives too fast reads as absence, and absence is billed as Tax."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "The ward runs on throughput. Beds, turns, targets."
THE COLLECTOR: "And yet the one thing a discharged person remembers is not your speed. It is whether someone looked up."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "I used to look up."
THE COLLECTOR: "I know. It is in the book, under a younger handwriting. The book don't blink, Priya. We are not inventing a new doctor. We are collecting the hurry that replaced her."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (+2) L 94 (+2) T 21 (—) │ PLT 72.4 ▲
DR. PRIYA NAIR: P 81 (-2) L 69 (+6) T 30 (-4) │ PLT 62.0 ▲
*Analyst: Priya gives up Profit here — the identity of 'fastest on the floor' — and her Love jumps six points in exchange. The trade is the lesson: some Profit is just Tax wearing a compliment.*

**Beat 4 — ONE PATIENT SLOW**
THE COLLECTOR: "The settlement will not be a retreat, or a course, or a poster in the lounge."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Good. I would have signed the poster without reading it."
THE COLLECTOR: "One patient. Today. On the page. You will sit, you will explain, you will ask the question like it has a question mark — and you will wait for the answer."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "One patient slow, while the pile—"
THE COLLECTOR: "The pile will survive one act of medicine, Doctor. It has survived everything else you do at speed."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Mr. Okafor, bed nine. His daughter has questions she has been saving. I have walked past them three times."
THE COLLECTOR: "Then we both know which patient."

DR. PRIYA NAIR: P 82 (+1) L 74 (+5) T 25 (-5) │ PLT 66.4 ▲
*Analyst: The prescription is deliberately small and performable — exactly one patient, slowed down on the record — because a Tax built by a thousand hurries is paid back one unhurried minute at a time.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
*Twenty minutes later. The printer is, for once, idle.*
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "He asked about the stairs at home. His daughter asked about night pain. I answered both, sitting down. It took eleven minutes and it was... the best medicine I have done all week."
THE COLLECTOR: "Name the debt with me, then, so the book can close it. Love withdrawn by hurry."
DR. PRIYA NAIR: "Love withdrawn by hurry. Named. And I am keeping the chair by bed nine exactly where it is."
THE COLLECTOR: "Collected. Settled. Your receipt — Bell Paws, tagged and registered. A small Betty Plush soul, Doctor. She never hurries. It is very restful."

DR. PRIYA NAIR: P 84 (+2) L 82 (+8) T 16 (-9) │ PLT 74.0 ▲

**THE MOVE — "OUTRUNNING EVERY FACE"**
*Dr. Priya Nair's play: Priya signed and discharged at such speed that no patient's face had time to become a name she would have to carry home. The maxim: "Hurry can protect the heart for a shift, but it cannot protect it for a life." The Collector's answer: ONE PATIENT SLOW — she prescribes a single seated patient, asked and answered unhurried, so one face is met fully and the hurry is collected instead of the doctor.*

**Takeaway:** *"Slowing down for one person is how you hear everyone again."*

## CHAPTER SIX — The Dealership & The Lot

### TC6.1 — The Financed Dream
*In a glass showroom at golden hour, balloons on every mirror and prices turned face-down in the windows: THE COLLECTOR in a red-and-black race-stripe fit under a leather jacket covered in Betty Plush patches, platform boots on the polished floor, her ornate scythe polished chrome-bright in the lot lights, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book in her hand — BETTY PLUSH 222 glowing over the doors, PLT stitched down her sleeve.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · RICO VANCE (car salesman, sells monthly payments instead of prices)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
RICO VANCE — P 45 · L 52 · T 68 │ PLT 25.6

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
RICO VANCE: "Welcome — don't look at the sticker, look at the smile it puts on you. What kind of monthly are we dreaming about today?"
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm not here to buy, Rico. I'm here to read. Pink book, your page. Profit 45. Love 52. Tax 68. PLT 25.6."
RICO VANCE: "Twenty-five six? I move units. I won a balloon last quarter."
THE COLLECTOR: "You move payments. You never say the whole price out loud, do you, Rico?"
RICO VANCE: "The whole price scares people off the lot."
THE COLLECTOR: "No. The whole price lets them leave on purpose. That's what scares you."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 90 (+2) T 20 (-2) │ PLT 70.0 ▲
RICO VANCE: P 43 (-2) L 50 (-2) T 72 (+4) │ PLT 22.4 ▼
*Analyst: A man who hides totals for a living hears his own total read whole — and it taxes him on the spot.*

**Beat 2 — THE PAYMENT TRICK**
RICO VANCE: "It's not hiding, it's translating. Nobody thinks in twenty-six thousand. Everybody thinks in a month."
THE COLLECTOR: "Translate one for me."
RICO VANCE: "Three-eighty-five a month — anybody can do three-eighty-five. Friendly. Fits in a week."
THE COLLECTOR: "Times how many months, Rico?"
RICO VANCE: "Eighty-four, but nobody —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Eighty-four. You cut one honest number into eighty-four little numbers so no hand ever holds the whole weight at once. That's not translation. That's slicing — and you call the slice a dream because 'debt' doesn't come with balloons."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (+2) L 90 (—) T 19 (-1) │ PLT 71.2 ▲
RICO VANCE: P 48 (+5) L 48 (-2) T 70 (-2) │ PLT 24.4 ▲
*Analyst: Rico's Profit ticks up when he explains his craft honestly — the trick is skilled work. His Love falls because skill aimed at confusion is still confusion, professionally delivered.*

**Beat 3 — SAME DREAM TWICE**
THE COLLECTOR: "Page two. A father bought here nine years ago. His son bought here last spring."
RICO VANCE: "Family business. They trust me."
THE COLLECTOR: "You sold his father the same dream in a different color. Same slice, same eighty-four, same speech about the smile. The son asked for you by name because his father said you were the man who helped him."
RICO VANCE: "And I did help —"
THE COLLECTOR: "You financed a man's trust twice and charged interest on both generations. He sent you his son, Rico. That's Love, deposited in your hands. You spent it as a closing technique."
RICO VANCE: "I never thought of it like that."
THE COLLECTOR: "That's the entry, baby. Not thinking of it like that is the whole Tax."
THE COLLECTOR: P 72 (—) L 94 (+4) T 18 (-1) │ PLT 73.2 ▲
RICO VANCE: P 46 (-2) L 55 (+7) T 66 (-4) │ PLT 28.0 ▲
*Analyst: Love rises for Rico the moment he sees the family as a family. The Collector doesn't invent his conscience — she itemises what it was already carrying.*

**Beat 4 — THE WHOLE NUMBER**
RICO VANCE: "So I walk up and say the big scary number and watch the lot empty?"
THE COLLECTOR: "You say the big honest number and watch the right people stay. Eighty-four pieces, and not one of them is the price. Say the price and the pieces become a plan. Hide the price and the plan becomes a trap. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you."
RICO VANCE: "The managers would eat me alive."
THE COLLECTOR: "The managers eat the customers alive, in slices, and hand you the knife with a balloon on it. Betty Plush 222 doesn't turn its prices to the wall, Rico. Neither will you."
RICO VANCE: "Whole number first. Then the monthly, if they want it."
THE COLLECTOR: P 75 (+3) L 95 (+1) T 17 (-1) │ PLT 75.2 ▲
RICO VANCE: P 44 (-2) L 60 (+5) T 58 (-8) │ PLT 32.4 ▲
*Analyst: Naming the whole number drops Rico's Tax by eight in one beat. The Tax was never the interest — it was the slicing, a choice he can unmake daily.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement time. The debt is named plainly: a Tax split into eighty-four pieces so nobody reads it whole, collected from two generations of one family and a lot full of strangers. It gets paid like this — the whole price spoken first, out loud, every window card turned face-out. Agree, and the book closes lighter."
RICO VANCE: "And if I slip? Old habits, chrome tongue —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Then you'll hear platform boots on your showroom floor again. Say how you'll sell, Rico."
RICO VANCE: "The whole price first, before the monthly. Every car, every family, every time."
THE COLLECTOR: "Collected. Here's your receipt — Pixel Heart, tagged and real, a small Betty Plush soul for a new start. Keep it on your desk. When a customer asks the total, answer like it's watching."
RICO VANCE: "It kind of is watching."
THE COLLECTOR: "They all are, baby. That's the point of a receipt. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 78 (+3) L 98 (+3) T 15 (-2) │ PLT 78.4 ▲
RICO VANCE: P 52 (+8) L 72 (+12) T 30 (-28) │ PLT 51.6 ▲
*Analyst: The biggest move is the Tax collapsing from 58 to 30 — eighty-four hidden slices named as one debt and paid in one sentence. Rico leaves still a salesman, but his first number is now the true one.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 78.4 · P 78 · L 98 · T 15 — ◆◆◆◆ "READ IT WHOLE"
RICO VANCE — PLT 51.6 · P 52 · L 72 · T 30 — ◆◆ "FINALLY SAW THE PRICE"

**THE MOVE — "THE MONTHLY MIRAGE"**
*Rico Vance's play: He split one big Tax into eighty-four small monthly payments so no customer ever had to read the whole price at once. The maxim: "Slice a price thin enough and nobody feels the weight until the last payment lifts it." The Collector's answer: THE WHOLE NUMBER FIRST — She makes him speak the full price out loud first and turn every window card face-out, so the pieces become a plan instead of a trap.*

**Takeaway:** *"A price cut into eighty-four pieces is still a price — somebody always pays it whole."*

### TC6.2 — The Trade-In Lie
*On the used row at midday, heat shimmering off hoods outside the appraisal office: THE COLLECTOR in a red-and-black race-stripe fit under a leather jacket covered in Betty Plush patches, platform boots on the cracked asphalt, her ornate scythe polished chrome-bright in the lot lights, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book open — BETTY PLUSH 222 painted down the office wall, PLT lettered on the price boards.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI (lot owner, under-values every trade-in)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI — P 62 · L 48 · T 58 │ PLT 34.8

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "If you're selling, I'm buying. If you're neither, honey, you're blocking a parking space."
THE COLLECTOR: "I'm collecting, B. Pink book, your page, out loud. Profit 62. Love 48. Tax 58. PLT 34.8."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "Thirty-four eight? I built this lot from one sedan and a folding chair."
THE COLLECTOR: "You built it, then you started appraising people at that desk instead of cars. You appraise who rolls it in, B — not what they roll in. Your Love is the lowest number in your row, and you own the row."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "I appraise what rolls in."
THE COLLECTOR: "We're going to read the difference, page by page. The book don't blink."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 90 (+2) T 20 (-2) │ PLT 70.4 ▲
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: P 60 (-2) L 46 (-2) T 62 (+4) │ PLT 31.6 ▼
*Analyst: B's Tax jumps as the Reading names her real method. She is not accused of bad arithmetic — she is accused of aiming good arithmetic at the wrong thing.*

**Beat 2 — READING THE OWNER**
THE COLLECTOR: "Walk me through a trade-in. Slow. I'm the lady with a dented hatchback and a baby seat in the back."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "Year, miles, tyres —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Before that."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "I look at whether she needs to sell. If she needs to sell, the car is worth less. That's the market wearing my face. You can tell by the shoes, by how fast they talk."
THE COLLECTOR: "There it is. A desperate owner makes a cheap car in your book, even if the engine is gold. Say it back to me, B, in your own voice."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "I read the owner instead of the car."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (+2) L 91 (+1) T 19 (-1) │ PLT 72.0 ▲
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: P 58 (-2) L 50 (+4) T 60 (-2) │ PLT 33.2 ▲
*Analyst: Her Profit dips as the technique is spoken plainly, but Love rises — naming what she does to people is the first time she has looked at the people in years.*

**Beat 3 — THE DESK WHERE LOVE LEFT**
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "You want to know when it started? When my husband died and the bank sent a man to appraise me. He looked at my shoes, Collector. Offered me half for the whole lot, cash, 'for my own good.'"
THE COLLECTOR: "And you kept the lot."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "I kept the lot and I kept his method. Told myself it was armour. Sit at that desk long enough and love withdraws itself — you just stop topping it up. Every low offer was me making sure nobody did it to me again."
THE COLLECTOR: "Love withdrawn at the valuation desk, and never redeposited. That's your debt, B — not the money you shaved. The money was just where the withdrawal showed. Grief with a calculator is still grief, baby. It just sends other people the bill."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (—) L 95 (+4) T 18 (-1) │ PLT 74.0 ▲
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: P 55 (-3) L 58 (+8) T 55 (-5) │ PLT 37.2 ▲
*Analyst: B's Tax is unspoken grief converted into under-valuations. Once the withdrawal is named, Love starts climbing back on its own.*

**Beat 4 — PRICE THE CAR**
THE COLLECTOR: "New rule. The next trade-in rolls in — what do you look at?"
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "The car. Year, miles, service book, tyres, engine. I price the car, not the custody battle, not the shoes, not the shaking hands."
THE COLLECTOR: "And if she needs to sell?"
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "Then she needs a fair number faster, not a smaller one slower. Needing help is not a discount code for the person across the desk."
THE COLLECTOR: "Say the short version, loud enough for the appraisal office."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "Price the car! Not the person! Fourteen staff are going to have questions about my personality change."

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement. The debt is named plainly: love withdrawn at the valuation desk, year after year, charged to strangers as low offers. It gets paid like this — every trade-in appraised on the car alone, the number written where the owner can read it, the first offer the honest one. Agree, and the book closes lighter."
MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI: "Agree. And I'll re-run last month's trade-ins myself. Anybody I read instead of their car gets a call and a corrected cheque."
THE COLLECTOR: "That's a redeposit with interest, B. Collected. Here's your receipt — Mendie, tagged and real, a small Betty Plush soul for a new start. She sits at the valuation desk from now on. Every offer, she watches."

MS. BARBARA "B" KOWALSKI — PLT 58.8 · P 64 · L 76 · T 28 — ◆◆◆ "LEARNED TO READ THE CAR"

**THE MOVE — "THE DESPERATION DISCOUNT"**
*Ms. Barbara "B" Kowalski's play: She read the owner instead of the car at the valuation desk, pricing a person's desperation rather than the machine in front of her. The maxim: "Desperation is not a trade-in value, no matter how fast you write it down." The Collector's answer: PRICE THE CAR, NOT THE PERSON — She makes B appraise every trade-in on the car alone, write the honest number where the owner can read it, and re-run a month of deals she priced off people.*

**Takeaway:** *"Appraise the car in front of you, not the hard year its owner drove in with."*

### TC6.3 — The Warranty
*In the finance office at the back of the showroom, blinds half-closed and a rack of brochures promising peace of mind in gold letters: THE COLLECTOR in a red-and-black race-stripe fit under a leather jacket covered in Betty Plush patches, platform boots either side of the paper-strewn desk, her ornate scythe polished chrome-bright in the lot lights by the door, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book open — BETTY PLUSH 222 on the office plaque, PLT across her sleeve.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · DEVON PRICE (finance manager, sells warranties for parts the cars don't have)

**OPENING HOLDING**

DEVON PRICE — P 68 · L 38 · T 62 │ PLT 31.6

THE COLLECTOR: "You'd sell a warranty to the Collector, Devon? Bold. Sit down. Pink book, your page. Profit 68. Love 38. Tax 62. PLT 31.6."
DEVON PRICE: "Profit sixty-eight is respectable, though."
THE COLLECTOR: "Profit 68, Love 38 is a man taking money faster than he's giving value, and the Tax keeps the receipts. Your Profit is the only number in this office that isn't nervous, baby. That should worry you."

DEVON PRICE: P 65 (-3) L 36 (-2) T 66 (+4) │ PLT 28.0 ▼

DEVON PRICE: "Covers the transmission, the drivetrain, the turbo assembly, the hybrid battery system, the —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Stop. The grey two-door you sold Tuesday. Does it have a turbo?"
DEVON PRICE: "It has… aspirations."
THE COLLECTOR: "A hybrid battery?"
DEVON PRICE: "The package is standard. Parts not included — I mean, the coverage is for parts the cars don't have because the package was designed for the range."
THE COLLECTOR: "You sold a man insurance on a turbo his car will never grow. That's not a package, baby. That's a poem about a car he doesn't own, and he pays monthly for the poetry. And you bank on nobody reading the schedule. Literally. It's in your Profit line."

DEVON PRICE: P 70 (+5) L 34 (-2) T 64 (-2) │ PLT 30.0 ▲
*Analyst: Devon's Profit ticks up even here — the packages genuinely sell. His Love keeps falling, because every defence is about the selling, never about the buyer.*

**Beat 3 — PEACE OF MIND, SOLD SEPARATELY**
DEVON PRICE: "Here's what you don't understand about this desk. People are scared. A car is the second-biggest thing they'll ever buy, and they sit there with their hands shaking. I sell them peace of mind. The peace is real even if —"
THE COLLECTOR: "Even if the parts aren't. Peace of mind, sold separately, for a machine that isn't in the car. If the peace were real, it would survive a breakdown. First time that two-door fails and your gold package pays out nothing, what does your customer feel?"
DEVON PRICE: "Betrayed."
THE COLLECTOR: "Betrayed is Love leaving the building with its bags packed. You didn't sell peace, baby. You rented it by the month, and the lease expires at the exact moment it's needed."
DEVON PRICE: "I told myself the calm at signing was the product. The calm was real, Collector."
THE COLLECTOR: "The calm was real. The stock was not. Profit taken without value — that's the entry, and it's been compounding at this desk for years. Name the Tax, or the Tax names you."
THE COLLECTOR: P 74 (—) L 94 (+4) T 18 (-1) │ PLT 74.0 ▲
DEVON PRICE: P 66 (-4) L 44 (+10) T 58 (-6) │ PLT 34.8 ▲
*Analyst: Devon's Love jumps ten points when he says "betrayed" himself. He always knew what the empty package would feel like at the roadside — he filed it under somebody else's department.*

**Beat 4 — THE EMPTY STOCKROOM**

DEVON PRICE: "There isn't one. There's a brochure rack and a printer that jams when a manager watches it."
THE COLLECTOR: "The peace was never in stock, Devon. You sold a thousand policies off an empty shelf and called the shelf a philosophy. What would this desk sell if it could only sell what the car actually has?"
DEVON PRICE: "Real coverage. Matched to the actual vehicle — its actual parts, its actual risks. And the man who bought the turbo poem gets refunded for the parts that don't exist. Every policy, audited against the car it was sold on."

It gets paid like this — every policy audited against the real car, refunds for every phantom part, and from today this desk sells only coverage that exists. Agree, and the book closes lighter."

DEVON PRICE — PLT 58.0 · P 68 · L 66 · T 24 — ◆◆◆ "SOLD ONLY WHAT EXISTS"

**THE MOVE — "THE EMPTY-SHELF LULLABY"**
*Devon Price's play: He sold peace of mind that was never in stock, writing warranties for parts the cars don't have and banking on nobody reading the schedule. **Takeaway:** *"You cannot sell peace of mind off an empty shelf — the customer finds out at the roadside."*

### TC6.4 — The Last Car on the Lot
*At closing time on the back row, away from the balloons, beside one old dusty-blue sedan with no price in its window, polished to a shine that has nothing to do with selling: THE COLLECTOR in a red-and-black race-stripe fit under a leather jacket covered in Betty Plush patches, platform boots quiet on the evening asphalt, her ornate scythe polished chrome-bright in the lot lights, Drippy the pink drip bunny plush in her arm, the grey three-eyed Spike bookbag on her back, the pink collection book held gently — BETTY PLUSH 222 glowing from the showroom, PLT catching the last light on her sleeve.*

**In the room:** THE COLLECTOR (soul collector, Soul Collection Agency) · SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ (old salesman, forty years on the lot, keeper of one unsellable car)

**OPENING HOLDING**
THE COLLECTOR — P 70 · L 88 · T 22 │ PLT 68.4
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ — P 55 · L 82 · T 38 │ PLT 53.6

**Beat 1 — THE READING**
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "We're closed, miss. Unless you're here about — no. Nobody's here about that one. That one's not for sale."
THE COLLECTOR: "I know, Sal. I'm collecting, and your page is a gentle one. Pink book, out loud. Profit 55. Love 82. Tax 38. PLT 53.6."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Fifty-three six. Forty years of selling and that's the number?"
THE COLLECTOR: "That's a good man's number. Look at that Love — eighty-two. You've been kind on this asphalt longer than most of these cars have existed. Your page is almost balanced."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Almost."
THE COLLECTOR: "Almost is why I'm here at closing time instead of noon. Small debts get quiet collection hours."
THE COLLECTOR: P 70 (—) L 90 (+2) T 21 (-1) │ PLT 69.6 ▲
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: P 54 (-1) L 80 (-2) T 40 (+2) │ PLT 51.6 ▼
*Analyst: Sal's score dips at the Reading not from shame but from nearness — he knows exactly which entry is coming, and he has been walking past it, polished, for years.*

**Beat 2 — FORTY YEARS ON THE ASPHALT**
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "I sold to fathers, then their kids, then — last spring — a granddaughter. I remember every family's driveway. Who needed the big boot for a wheelchair, who needed cheap because the plant closed."
THE COLLECTOR: "Forty years on the asphalt, and you never sold cars, Sal. You placed them, like furniture in people's lives."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Everybody on this lot carries something heavier than a down payment. The man who carried everybody else's never once sat in the customer's chair. You sit in that chair, you admit you need something. I was the lot's furniture, miss. Furniture doesn't need."
THE COLLECTOR: "Furniture gets polished and never gets asked how it is. Keep going, Sal. You're doing my job for me."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (+1) L 92 (+2) T 20 (-1) │ PLT 71.2 ▲
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: P 56 (+2) L 82 (+2) T 38 (-2) │ PLT 54.0 ▲
*Analyst: Sal's Love is already high and stays high — this collection is not about love withheld from others. His care only ever travelled in one direction: outward.*

**Beat 3 — THE POLISHED ONE**
THE COLLECTOR: "Tell me about the car with no price in the window. The one shining like a church on a row that gets no balloons."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "That was my brother's. Marco. He bought it off this lot the year I started — paid me my first commission and made me buy him lunch with it, the thief. When he passed, his wife asked me to sell it. I said yes. I have been saying yes for nine years."
THE COLLECTOR: "And polishing it every Friday for nine years. The polished one is the only appointment you keep."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Every buyer who ever asked, I found a flaw to tell them. Engine's tired. Wiring's moody. I lied about a good car to keep a dead man's seat warm. You can't sell a car you're still having a conversation with, Collector."
THE COLLECTOR: "You didn't keep it because it's a car, baby. You kept it because polishing is the only grief you ever let yourself buy."
THE COLLECTOR: P 71 (—) L 96 (+4) T 19 (-1) │ PLT 73.2 ▲
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: P 55 (-1) L 88 (+6) T 36 (-2) │ PLT 56.8 ▲
*Analyst: Sal's Love rises sharply because the polishing is finally named as what it always was — devotion, not inventory. The car was never stock. It was a standing appointment with his brother.*

**Beat 4 — NEVER THE CUSTOMER**
THE COLLECTOR: "Here's the entry, Sal, small and old. You never let yourself be a customer of your own grief. Forty years you sold comfort — rides home, fair deals, a chair for every frightened buyer — and never once let anybody sell you the thing you needed."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "And what's that? What does the lot sell for a man like me?"
THE COLLECTOR: "Permission to be finished. To say: this hurts, it has hurt for nine years, and I am allowed to be the one who is helped today. Never the customer — that was the Tax, Sal. Not big. Just charged every Friday, in polish."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Marco would laugh at me. 'Salvatore, you kept my car and lost the plot.' Then he'd make me buy lunch."
THE COLLECTOR: "Then buy the lunch, baby. That's a debt that pays both ways."
THE COLLECTOR: P 73 (+2) L 97 (+1) T 18 (-1) │ PLT 74.8 ▲
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: P 54 (-1) L 92 (+4) T 32 (-4) │ PLT 59.6 ▲
*Analyst: The Tax here is tiny by the chapter's standards, and that is the point — a small unnamed charge, paid weekly for nine years, still bends a whole ledger.*

**Beat 5 — THE SETTLEMENT**
THE COLLECTOR: "Settlement, Sal, and it's the softest one in this book. The debt is named plainly: nine years of grief you never let yourself be a customer of — small, old, paid in polish every Friday. It gets paid like this — the car goes to Marco's granddaughter, who asked about it last spring. You call her yourself. And your Fridays you spend at his table now, being fed instead of shining."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "She did ask. She has his laugh, Collector. The car should hear that laugh from the driver's seat."
THE COLLECTOR: "Collected. Here's your receipt — Nightwing Dawn, tagged and real, a small Betty Plush soul for a new start. She rides in the car on the drive over. A soul delivered, for the man who delivered everybody else's."
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: "Forty years I handed people keys. First time somebody's handed me something back."
THE COLLECTOR: "You were always worth handing something to, Sal. Walk me out, old-timer. Lot's closed — and for once, so is your page. Balanced. Settled."
THE COLLECTOR: P 75 (+2) L 100 (+3) T 16 (-2) │ PLT 77.6 ▲
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ: P 60 (+6) L 96 (+4) T 18 (-14) │ PLT 69.2 ▲
*Analyst: The Tax drop — 32 to 18 — is Sal's largest move: the whole debt was the Tax, and it clears when he agrees to receive instead of only give.*

**FINAL SCORE**
THE COLLECTOR — PLT 77.6 · P 75 · L 100 · T 16 — ◆◆◆◆ "EVERY PAGE, READ GENTLY"
SALVATORE "SAL" RUIZ — PLT 69.2 · P 60 · L 96 · T 18 — ◆◆◆ "FINALLY SAT IN THE CHAIR"

**THE MOVE — "THE POLISHED VIGIL"**
*Salvatore "Sal" Ruiz's play: He kept one unsellable car polished and priced out of reach so grief always had a post on the lot and he never had to clock out of losing his brother. The maxim: "A ritual can hold a memory, but it cannot hold the clock still." The Collector's answer: GIVE THE KEYS TO THE LAUGHTER — She has him hand the car to Marco's granddaughter who asked for it, trade Friday polishing for Fridays at his table, and finally sit in the customer's chair himself.*

**Takeaway:** *"Even the man who carries everyone has to sit in the customer's chair sometime."*
